#201. CEOs are tired of being held responsible for gun regulation

Americans have grown used to corporate executives treading the well-worn paths of the Northeast corridor to convene alongside elected officials in Washington, DC, and discuss geopolitics, policy and all that’s in-between.

In 2017, major CEOs from across the country came together to oppose North Carolina’s transgender bathroom law. In 2019, they called abortion bans “bad for business.”

After the deadly attack on the US Capitol on January 6, 2021, many of corporate America’s biggest names denounced the rioters and pledged to halt their political giving.

Recently, more than 1,000 companies promised to voluntarily curtail their operations in Russia in protest of Moscow’s war on Ukraine.

Dick’s Sporting Goods stopped selling semi-automatic, assault-style rifles at stores and Citigroup put new restrictions on gun sales by business customers after the mass shooting at a high school in Parkland, Florida, in 2018.

A year later, after mass shootings at a Walmart in El Paso, Texas, and a nightclub in Dayton, Ohio, Walmart ended handgun ammunition sales.

Corporate leadership has long been vocal on the issue of gun control – in 2019 and again this past summer nearly 150 major companies – including Lululemon, Lyft, Bain Capital, Bloomberg LP, Permanente Medical Group and Unilever – called gun violence a “public health crisis” and demanded that the US Senate pass legislation to address it.

That’s why corporate America’s silence in the wake of the latest mass shooting at a school in Nashville is so jarring. The United States has come to rely on the increasing power of large corporations as political advocates.

But Yale professor Jeffrey Sonnenfeld, a vocal advocate of corporate social responsibility who has a direct line to major CEOs around the globe, said that top executives are forlorn. Their previous efforts haven’t done much to push the needle on gun control legislation and without more backing, they don’t know what else they can do at the moment, he said.

Before the Bell spoke with Sonnenfeld, who runs Yale School of Management’s Chief Executive Leadership Institute, a nonprofit educational and research institute focused on CEO leadership and corporate governance.

This interview has been edited for clarity and length.

Before the Bell: CEOs have been quiet about gun reform since the latest mass school shooting in Nashville, have you heard anything about plans to speak out?

Jeffrey Sonnenfeld: Where is everybody else? Where is all of civil society? CEOs are just one group of people and it’s like we’re turning to them to be our saviors on every topic. They’ve joined causes with valor and nobility but they can’t just be taking cause after cause as if there’s nobody else in society. The social change that happened in the 1960s wasn’t being led primarily by CEOs. Social changes really happened when we saw the interfaith activity of clergy locking arms and canvassing legislators. We saw campuses alive and aroused. Where’s all the student activism?

The CEOs are still the most active even if they’re less active than they were six months ago. They’re not there as hired hands of shareholders to fill the role of politicians and civic leaders. They’re there to join that chorus, but they don’t want to be the only one singing.

So is this what you’re hearing from top CEOs? Have they gotten tired of advocating?

I just got off of a CEO call on voting rights and this morning we had a forum on sustainability – CEOs are still the most active on these fronts. It’s the same thing on immigration reform. If a CEO was working an 18 hour day on a 12 day week, they still couldn’t address all of the issues that need addressing.

The nation’s CEOs are waiting for everybody else to join them. They don’t need to restate something they’ve already stated. They’ve jumped in the pool, where’s everybody else?

So what do you think has led to this complacency amongst Americans and the growing reliance on CEOs to advocate on our behalf?

They’ve taken a very strong stance and they’ve gone out further than the general public. They are where the general public is on surveys, but they’re not where the general public is on action in the streets. So we’re ready for others to now do something. Enough already on saying ‘what are the CEOs doing?’ Social capital is as valuable as financial capital. CEOs understand that in their soul, they want there to be social capital. They want there to be public trust, but they need the rest of civil society to join them. And that’s their frustration.

But don’t these CEOs hold the purse strings in terms of donating to powerful politicians?

You would think that, but since the 2020 elections much less of campaign contributions have come from big business. Since the 2021 run on the Capitol, a lot of businesses either had an official moratorium or they’ve given mere pennies to politicians. The common impression on the street that CEOs are controlling campaign purses strings is 100% wrong.

Tesla reported. a modest 4% rise in sales in the first quarter compared to the final three months of last year, despite a series of price cuts on its lower priced vehicles and talk by CEO Elon Musk about strong demand at those lower prices.

The first quarter also marked the fourth straight quarter that Tesla has produced more vehicles than it has delivered to customers. Some of that may be due to the ramp up in production at two new factories, one in Texas, the other in Germany, which opened last spring, and a lag between that increased production and sales.

Tesla said there was an increase in the number of its more expensive models, the Model S and Model X, in transit to Europe, the Middle East and Africa, as well as to the Asia Pacific region.

But it does mean that over the last 12 months Tesla has produced 78,000 more cars than it has sold, suggesting that talk of strong demand by Tesla executives may not be backed up by the numbers.

“Early this year, we had a price adjustment. After that, we actually generated a huge demand, more than we can produce, really,” said Tom Zhu, Tesla’s executive in charge of global production and sales. “And as Elon said, as long as you offer a product with value at affordable price, you don’t have to worry about demand.”

#196. As Van Hollen wages a challenge, Senate Democrats are torn on whom to elect as their leader

Maryland senator’s bid against Chuck Schumer for leadership could decide direction of the Democratic party

Ever since Donald Trump returned to the White House, Democratic officeholders and voters alike have been divided over whether their leader in the Senate, Chuck Schumer, is up to the task of taking the president on.

That debate intensified on Thursday, when the Maryland senator Chris Van Hollen announced he will challenge Schumer for the post of Democratic Senate leader, which will become one of the most powerful jobs in Washington when the party takes control of Congress’s upper chamber next year.

The contest between Schumer and Van Hollen is the party’s first major test of unity after its triumph in last week’s midterm elections, in which voters gave Democrats their biggest majorities in both the Senate and House of Representatives in years. Now the party will be forced to confront the two veteran lawmakers’ differing visions on how to use their newly expanded power to confront Trump, advance the party’s agenda and address divisive issues such as the question of aid to Israel.

“Trump’s policies have been fired, but we are still in the position where we need to take advantage of this opportunity. So that does mean closing the trust deficit? … And I don’t think you can close the trust deficit by simply returning to the status quo,” Van Hollen said at a press conference on Friday.

Progressive groups have cheered the 67-year-old Van Hollen’s candidacy, and he’s picked up a handful of endorsements in the hours since he made his bid official. But his prospects are unclear: the senator said he is an “underdog” in the contest, which is set to be decided at a behind-closed-doors caucus meeting scheduled for 1 December.

Schumer, meanwhile, has expressed confidence he’ll win the Democratic caucus’s nod again, and has the support of senators from across the party’s ideological spectrum.

“I welcome any discussion about the future of our caucus and the important work ahead,” Schumer, 75, wrote on X after Van Hollen announced his bid. “I have the votes to continue serving as Democratic Leader.”

A New York City native, Schumer won election to the House in 1980, then the Senate in 1998. He became the top Senate Democrat in 2017, when the party was in the minority at the beginning of Trump’s first term. Four years later, Schumer became majority leader under Joe Biden, and managed to win passage of parts of his legislative agenda even though the Democratic caucus was so small it could not lose a single vote on party-line bills.

Trump returned to power in 2025, and it was not long after that Schumer’s leadership came into question. In March of that year, Congress faced a deadline to approve new government funding or cause a shutdown. Though many Democrats believed they had leverage over Trump to win concessions, perhaps over the gutting of the federal government by the “department of government efficiency”, Schumer opted to advance a GOP-backed spending measure, arguing that it was a bad time to shut down the government.

The compromise triggered an explosion of anger at Schumer both from Democrats in the House of Representatives who had rejected the funding compromise, and activist groups who saw it as a sign that Schumer was no longer fit to be leader.

“Senator Schumer has contributed to and led many important accomplishments that Indivisible is grateful for. But with our democracy on the line, he let us, the country and the Democratic party down,” said Ezra Levin, co-executive director of the network of progressive groups.

“Senator Schumer should step aside as leader. Every Democrat in the Senate should call for him to do so, and begin making plans for new leadership immediately.”

The relationship between Schumer and the party base – including some Democratic lawmakers – has yet to recover. Another wave of calls for Schumer to step down arrived later that year, when dissident Senate Democrats decided to end a record-long government shutdown without Republicans meeting any of their demands on healthcare.

Schumer did not publicly endorse their deal, but it was widely believed to have his backing. By the time campaigning for the 2026 midterms began, several candidates were making their opposition to him prominent in their platforms.

In some ways, the tension is a unique consequence of the differences in power between the minority party in the House and Senate. In the former, the party out of power has little recourse to block legislation, but in the latter, the filibuster’s 60-vote threshold makes Schumer a major figure in deciding whether his party plays along with the 53 Republicans in the majority, or obstructs them.

At the same time that Schumer’s standing among Democrats was growing rocky, Van Hollen’s star was ascending. Elected to the Senate from the House in 2016, Van Hollen made national news when he traveled to El Salvador to meet Kilmar Ábrego García, a Salvadorian who had been wrongfully deported from Maryland in the early days of Trump’s mass deportation campaign.

A deep dive into the policies, controversies and oddities surrounding the Trump administration

He has joined in criticizing Israel, amid a wider backlash among Democratic voters towards the US ally fueled by accusations it committed a genocide in Gaza. Van Hollen voted against providing bulldozers and bombs to the country, and after a visit to the Gaza-Egypt border in 2025, co-released a report accusing Israel of planning to ethnically cleanse the territory. In September, the senator led an unsuccessful resolution to force the state department to issue a report on violence in the West Bank.

Schumer’s criticism of Israel has been comparatively muted – he did not vote for the efforts to cute off arm sales, but did support Van Hollen’s resolution on the state department report. When he was majority leader in 2024, and thereby the highest-ranking Jewish official in the US government, Schumer called on the Israeli prime minister, Benjamin Netanyahu, to step aside.

Last week’s sweep of the midterm elections seems to vindicate his leadership of Senate Democrats – after all, the party won just about every state it was contesting, and some where it had invested few resources. Yet Van Hollen said Schumer could have done better, noting that several candidates he had backed in the primaries wound up being rejected by voters.

After the election, reports emerged that Van Hollen was not planning to challenge Schumer, fueling speculation that he would instead run for president in 2028. He now says he’s not interested in a presidential run, and announced the bid for party leader just as activists again called for Senate Democrats to replace Schumer.

“Our members are tired of politicians content with incrementalism – they want bold, creative leadership that will help Democrats usher in affordability and accountability without delay or compromise in the next Congress,” said Joel Payne, the executive director of liberal group MoveOn.

“We welcome and will support any potential Senate leader willing to meet the moment.”

A small number of Democratic senators have publicly backed Van Hollen since he announced his leadership bid, including Bernie Sanders, the independent progressive who caucuses with the party, and incoming senators Abdul El-Sayed of Michigan and Peggy Flanagan of Minnesota. Others, such as Elissa Slotkin of Michigan, incoming senator Adam Hamilton of Kansas and Juliana Stratton of Illinois, said the party should choose a new leader, but have not said who that should be.

Schumer, meanwhile, retains considerable sway in the party, and has been endorsed by progressive and moderate senators alike. Those who have backed him argue he’s been effective, and point to last week’s results as proof.

“Why would you fire the manager when you just won the World Series?” asked Angus King, the Maine independent who caucuses with the Democrats, and endorsed Schumer.

#194. New Units of Knowledge

#193. Tanker collides with cruise ship on Mississippi River in New Orleans

Large emergency response under way to rescue people from the water, with overnight cruise ship on its side

New Orleans river collision – latest updates

A tanker collided with a river cruise ship docked on the Mississippi River in New Orleans on Friday afternoon, prompting an emergency effort to rescue people from the water, according to officials.

The Atlantic Asphalt tanker collided with American Cruise Lines’ American Heritage at about 12.30pm, said Kimberly Curth, the Port of New Orleans press secretary, in a statement.

The American Heritage had about 129 passengers – in addition to crew members – onboard when it was hit, according to Curth. About two hours before that, the American Heritage had docked at the port to begin passenger disembarkation, Curth said.

A statement from the office of the New Orleans mayor, Helena Moreno, on Friday afternoon said that “multiple individuals have been rescued”.

An emergency response source said that there were efforts under way – including by New Orleans firefighters – to rescue multiple people from the water.

“Public safety agencies are coordinating response efforts, with the immediate priority being the safety and rescue of those involved,” the statement from Moreno’s office said.

Officials did not immediately provide details about the circumstances of the crash. The emergency response source said it was possible the tanker had lost power before striking the American Heritage, but that had not been confirmed.

The website marinetraffic.com showed the Atlantic Asphalt’s path was uneventful as it went around the Mississippi River bend near New Orleans’ Algiers Point neighborhood. It had just made it past the Crescent City Connection bridge linking New Orleans’ central business district and the Algiers section when it began drifting to its starboard – or right – side.

Video showed it hit the side of the American Heritage, which slowly rolled toward where it was struck. One witness, Jamie Holford, told local NBC News affiliate WDSU that it took 15 minutes “from impact to where it rolled over”.

“Surreal – that’s what that was,” Holford said.

Maritime law expert Martin J Davies of New Orleans’ Tulane University told local Fox affiliate WVUE that the Atlantic Asphalt was lucky to have not hit the Crescent City Connection, which is estimated to carry about 160,000 cars daily.

“This is obviously a really bad, incident,” Davies said. “But it could also have struck the bridge, which it didn’t.”

A video from WDSU showed crew members of a tugboat working to pull people in lifejackets or holding on to flotation rings out of the river.

Video from WVUE showed the American Heritage slowly rolling on to its side with the Atlantic Asphalt nearby. One image on social media showed people climbing on the side of the cruise boat – specifically, on stateroom balconies – after it was struck, with chairs and tables strewn about.

People who had gotten off the American Heritage after it was struck were being brought inside the New Orleans port’s administration building.

The operator of New Orleans’ University Medical Center, LCMC, reported on Friday that there were 24 patients admitted to the hospital from the crash. The local Acadian Ambulance service took eight people from there to the hospital to be treated for injuries, the details of which were not immediately available, agency spokesperson Caitlin Russo said.

Rescue-related activity on the river had significantly scaled back later on Friday afternoon in the hours after the crash.

Beside New Orleans firefighters and port and city police, the US Coast Guard and local emergency medical services personnel were at the scene conducting rescue and response operations, Curth’s statement said.

A statement from the US Coast Guard said the river “is closed to all vessel traffic between mile markers 93 and 96”, encompassing the area where Friday’s crash occurred. That is so to assist responders as they “assess the situation and conduct response operations”, the statement said.

A statement from American Cruise Lines on Friday said “guests and crew were evacuated” from the American Heritage but did not provide numbers. “We are deeply concerned for everyone involved,” the cruise operator’s statement added.

The American Heritage, a 300ft paddle-wheel ship, was built in 2015 and previously sailed as the Queen of the Mississippi. It has more than 80 staterooms and operates cruises on the Mississippi River, including ones which depart from New Orleans.

The Atlantic Asphalt is a oil tanker built in 2017 measuring approximately 160 meters in length and has a deadweight of 17,764 metric tons. It flies the flag of the Marshall Islands and is owned and commercially operated by Nave Shipping SA, a company registered in Liberia. Its maintenance, technical and safety operations are overseen by Singapore-based Thome Ship Management, according to online records.

The vessel, which had a next recorded destination for Singapore, does not appear on the international sanctions lists checked by maritime tracking services, including Magic Port.

However, it was previously fined C$7,800 by Canadian authorities for a violation of the Canada Shipping Act and related vessel pollution regulations. The penalty, recorded on 11 August 2020, stemmed from an officer failing to properly carry out their duties while supervising a transfer operation aboard the oil tanker, according to the Canadian government.

Louisiana’s governor, Jeff Landry, on Friday afternoon said he was headed to New Orleans.

#190. HSBC's top execs face tense shareholders calling for a breakup

HSBC’s top brass defended their strategy Monday to frustrated shareholders in the lender’s largest market, as Europe’s biggest bank continued to face calls to be split up.

At an informal shareholder meeting in Hong Kong, Chairman Mark Tucker and CEO Noel Quinn took questions from investors on issues ranging from how the bank was approaching demands for an overhaul of its business to its purchase of Silicon Valley Bank’s UK arm.

In prepared remarks, Tucker and Quinn each reiterated the board’s recommendation that shareholders vote against a resolution on the docket for its annual general meeting in May that would force the bank to come up with a plan to spin off or reorganize its Asian business — the lender’s main source of profits.

HSBC's biggest shareholder reignites debate about breaking up the bank

Tucker said the board was unanimous in its opposition to the resolution, stating plainly: “It would not be in your interest to split the bank.”

He said the board had previously reviewed a range of options for restructuring the bank, and concluded that such alternatives would “materially destroy value for shareholders,” including dividends.

“Our strategy is working,” Tucker told the room of more than 1,000 shareholders. “Our current strategy is moving dividends up.”

HSBC has been facing calls to separate its Asian business from the rest of the bank over the past year.

Shareholders in Hong Kong — where HSBC is a mainstay of many retail investors’ portfolios — contend that the London-based lender’s performance has been dragged down by its businesses in other regions.

Quinn addressed those complaints head-on Monday, saying “our profits in Hong Kong and the UK are no longer being dragged down by underperformance elsewhere. The group is performing well as a whole.”

Pressed later by a shareholder on the issue, Quinn said a breakup of the bank would result in “significant revenue loss” because much of its business relied on cross-border transactions.

Investors have also been unhappy with HSBC scrapping its dividend in 2020, at the request of British regulators. They argue that if the lender cordoned off its activities in Asia, it would no longer have to expose Hong Kong shareholders to requests in other jurisdictions.

Christine Fong, a district council member in Hong Kong, said she represented about 500 small shareholders who had been affected by the dividend cancellation.

“Street hawkers, taxi drivers or teachers — they all relied on the dividend to pay for their regular expenses, like mortgage, insurance payments, school fees,” Fong told CNN.

“That’s why, three years ago, what HSBC did upset those small minority shareholders.”

Fong has now joined calls for shareholders to vote in favor of the proposal for the bank to spin off its Asian business, despite the lender bringing back its dividend in 2021, albeit at a lower level.

Ken Lui, an activist shareholder in Hong Kong who put the resolution together, doubled down on his call for support ahead of the meeting Monday.

The resolution will require 75% of votes to be passed in May, but “nothing is impossible,” he told reporters outside the meeting venue.

Lui, who said he personally held a stake worth 100 million Hong Kong dollars ($12.7 million), laid out plans for his team to focus on “targeted outreach to institutional shareholders to present our case and gain their support.”

His group will also canvass 18 districts of Hong Kong “to tell HSBC shareholders that they finally have a chance to speak for themselves and protect their rights through voting,” he added.

HSBC is also facing pressure from its largest shareholder.

Ping An (PNGAY), China’s biggest insurer, holds an 8% stake in HSBC and has backed calls for the bank to rethink its structure.

In a series of remarks made public by the Chinese firm last November, Huang Yong, chairman of Ping An’s asset management arm, said “we will support any initiatives including a spinoff that are conducive to improve HSBC’s performance and value.”

Since then, the insurance giant’s views haven’t changed, according to a person familiar with the matter.

The source told CNN that Ping An has been calling for HSBC to explore a reorganization, with an eye on boosting its valuation and simplifying its regulatory obligations around the globe.

The insurer has not recommended a specific path forward but will support any initiatives, including a spinoff of its Asian business, that could boost its stock performance or value, the person added. Ping An did not immediately respond to a request for comment on how it planned to vote at the upcoming general meeting.

HSBC’s leaders were also asked Monfday why the bank had scooped up the British unit of SVB following the stunning collapse of its parent in the United States. The purchase was made for £1 ($1.20) last month, just days after SVB had folded.

Critics have questioned HSBC’s ability to perform adequate due diligence on SVB UK’s customers because of how quickly the deal came together.

“Did HSBC look into the clients of SVB in detail? Say, the financial statement — whether they can pay back the loan?” said Fong.

HSBC buys SVB's UK business, ending 'nightmare' for British tech

Quinn and Tucker defended the acquisition, calling it a good business opportunity that allowed the bank to gain hundreds of innovative startups as customers. They pushed back on the notion that management hadn’t had time to carry out proper due diligence.

Tucker also weighed in on recent tumult in the banking industry, saying he did not expect an “immediate impact” on HSBC.

“After the collapse of a number of smaller regional banks and the takeover of Credit Suisse, the share prices of all banks have been suppressed,” he noted.

But he said he did not believe such developments represented “a systemic risk” to the sector. “I do expect a period of uncertainty” before nerves settle, he added.

#187. Nationwide FAA ground stop: nearly 11,000 flights delayed or canceled at airports across the US

Transportation secretary said stoppage is due to ‘communication issue’ and homeland security says there’s no indication of ‘nefarious attack’

Flights grounded at all US airports due to communications outage – live

The Federal Aviation Administration (FAA) has issued a nationwide ground stop for all flights that are due to take off in the US on Friday, delaying or canceling more than 11,000 US flights, according to aviation tracker FlightAware.

In a post on X, Sean Duffy, the US transportation secretary, said the stoppage is due to “a communication issue impacting all Air Route Traffic Control Centers” and that the FAA’s technicians are looking into the matter.

A notice from the FAA said the ground stop was being imposed “due to extraordinary circumstances and for reasons of safety”.

Markwayne Mullin, the Department of Homeland security director, said in a news conference that preliminary investigations into the stoppage do not indicate that it was tied to a “nefarious attack”.

“There’s no indication as of yet that this was a nefarious attack against it. It’s just a telecommunications outage as of right now,” he told reporters.

After restoring communications so all airborne flights can land, Mullin said, “we’re going to continue to work to find out what the root of the cause is”.

“We’re not ruling out anything at this point,” Mullin added.

Mullin said the immediate priority was ensuring that planes still in the air would be landing safely, but that there was no clear information on how long the stoppage might last.

In a statement, the Los Angeles mayor, Karen Bass, said that she was aware of the stoppage and getting updates on it. Los Angeles is home to LAX, the fifth-busiest airport in the US, according to the bureau of transportation statistics.

“I am in close contact with leadership at Los Angeles World Airports (LAWA) to receive real-time updates and assess impacts. We will continue to monitor this evolving situation and work closely with federal and state partners.”

At Ronald Reagan Washington national airport, gate agents, along with most travelers in the terminal, refreshed their phones looking for updates, and attempted to change their flights. Nearly every seat was filled, with some people sitting on the floor and using their luggage as makeshift desks for their laptops.

A ground-stop order by the FAA requires that aircraft remain on the ground and bars them from taking off. Planes already in the air are usually allowed to remain in the air. This is the third time all departing flights have been grounded in the US. The first time was on 11 September 2001; the other was in January 2023, when the FAA paused all ⁠domestic departures for less than two hours after a pilot notification system malfunctioned.

The FAA has more than 14,000 air traffic controllers who handle the more than 44,000 flights that take off from airports across the US each day, according to FAA data.

Last week, the FAA announced that the first digital air traffic control towers in the US are officially operational in Alaska and Florida, as part of its ongoing modernization project that is meant to reduce flight delays and improve the flow of air traffic.

Last year, ​Congress ​approved $12.5bn to fix ​the FAA’s ageing communications system, and ​on Friday ‌the FAA ​and ​White House asked Congress for another $30.1bn to address other issues.

#184. China Renaissance suspends trading, delays results after founder's disappearance

China Renaissance, a top dealmaker in the country’s tech industry, said it would suspend trading of its shares and delay the release of its annual results because it still can’t get in touch with its founder.

Bao Fan, 52, started the boutique investment bank in 2005 and has been unreachable since the middle of February, according to the company. Shares in China Renaissance have plunged since Bao went missing, at one point dropping as much as 50%.

China Renaissance said in late February that it had learned Bao was “cooperating in an investigation” being carried out by certain authorities in the country. It gave no other details.

Chinese media have reported Bao might be assisting in an investigation related to a former executive at China Renaissance.

In a filing on Sunday, China Renaissance said auditors couldn’t complete their work or sign off on their report because of Bao’s absence. The board was also unable to give an estimate about when it would be able to approve its audited results for 2022 or dispatch its annual report by an April 30 deadline as required by Hong Kong’s listing rules.

Missing Chinese billionaire CEO assisting authorities in investigation, company says

Trading in the company’s shares was suspended from Monday as a result.

Bao is known as a veteran dealmaker who works closely with top technology companies in China. He helped broker the 2015 merger between two of the country’s leading food delivery services, Meituan and Dianping. Today, the combined company’s “super app” platform is ubiquitous in China.

His team has also invested in US-listed Chinese electric vehicle makers Nio (NIO) and Li Auto and helped Chinese internet giants Baidu (BIDU) and JD.com (JD) complete their secondary listings in Hong Kong.

Over the weekend, China’s top anti-graft watchdog launched an investigation into Liu Liange, former party secretary and chairman of Bank of China, according to a statement by the Central Commission for Discipline Inspection and the State Supervision Commission. The bank is state-owned and one of the country’s four biggest lenders.

Liu is suspected of “serious violations of discipline and law,” the statement said. He is among the most senior financial executives targeted in a broader financial crackdown by President Xi Jinping.

In January, Wang Bin, former party chief and chairman of China Life Insurance, was charged by national-level prosecutors with taking bribes and hiding overseas savings.

#181. New Orleans boat crash live: all passengers and crew found alive in ‘miracle on the Mississippi’

New Orleans mayor says 32 people are being treated for minor injuries after ship narrowly missed striking a major bridge

Tanker collides with cruise ship on Mississippi River in New Orleans

New Orleans mayor Helena Moreno said all passengers and crew have been accounted for in what she called “truly a miracle on the Mississippi.”

She added the ship narrowly missed striking a bridge carrying around 160,000 vehicles daily.

Thirty-two people are now being treated at a local hospital. Those include two New Orleans Harbor Police officers who went into the water to rescue people after the cruise ship began sinking.

A tanker collided with a river cruise liner, the American Cruise Lines’ American Heritage, carrying 129 passengers plus crew in New Orleans on Friday. It began turning on its side and taking on water.

All passengers and crew are alive and have now been accounted for in what New Orleans mayor Helena Moreno called “truly a miracle on the Mississippi.”

It is unclear exactly what caused the collision. Melanie Burnham, commander of the US Coast Guard sector in New Orleans, told reporters that “there seems to be some type of a loss of steering,” in regards to the tanker.

Louisiana governor Jeff Landry, addressing reporters during a news conference, said he will be launching an investigation into what led to the collision to ensure it will not happen again.

Moreno said the tanker ship narrowly missed striking a bridge carrying around 160,000 vehicles daily.

Thirty-two people are being treated at a local hospital. Those include two New Orleans Harbor Police officers who went into the water to rescue people after the cruise ship began sinking.

Several photos and videos circulating around social media show different angles during the moment the ship was falling on its side into the water. Passengers were seen climbing onto the balconies’ railing to avoid sliding into the river as the ship turned horizontally.

Louisiana governor Jeff Landry, addressing reporters during a news conference, said he will be launching an investigation into what led to the collision to ensure it will not happen again.

Landry said the Mississippi river is “not some quiet pond” and praised state and local officials for their quick response to the incident. He added that first responders risked their lives to save those in the water.

Captain Melanie Burnham, commander of the US Coast Guard sector in New Orleans, told reporters that “There seems to be some type of a loss of steering,” in regards to the tanker.

“The exact reason why - we don’t know that,” she said.

“University Medical Center New Orleans and other LCMC Health hospitals have received an estimated two dozen patients following the reported maritime incident,” LCMC said in a statement shared with media outlets. “Our medical teams are currently evaluating patients and providing appropriate care while coordinating with emergency responders.”

An employee at the Convention Center, who did not want to be named, said she was on the ground floor when she looked up and noticed that, beyond the brick wall that separates the building from the Mississippi, the very tip of the river cruise ship was tilted. Then, she heard screaming. “People were screaming for help,” she said.

She and colleagues assembled wheelchairs and water and other items to be sent in the direction of survivors. “I’m just glad I didn’t see anything, I already hold this deep fear of water.”

New Orleans mayor Helena Moreno said all passengers and crew have been accounted for in what she called “truly a miracle on the Mississippi.”

She added the ship narrowly missed striking a bridge carrying around 160,000 vehicles daily.

Thirty-two people are now being treated at a local hospital. Those include two New Orleans Harbor Police officers who went into the water to rescue people after the cruise ship began sinking.

Witnesses in New Orleans described a scene of chaos as the liner listed on its side and took on water.

Devon Flanagan was at his job nearby at the Port of New Orleans when workers began rushing down the dock. He got out of his truck and noticed the American Heritage on its side and passengers in the water.

“It was just chaos,” he said. “You had all types of ships, like other barges and other companies down there trying to help out, which also was a little convoluted because you had people still in the water.”

“Surreal — that’s what that was,” witness Jamie Holford told WDSU-TV. “Fifteen minutes from impact to where it rolled over.”

A tanker collided with a river cruise liner, the American Cruise Lines’ American Heritage, carrying 129 passengers plus crew in New Orleans on Friday.

The cruise liner was taking on water as it rapidly turned on its side, and videos showed people climbing on to the railing to avoid falling in the water.

New Orleans mayor Helena Moreno’s communication team said multiple individuals have been rescued, and search-and-rescue operations remain ongoing. Helicopters were seen in the air along with ambulances waiting nearby.

It is currently unclear if there are any fatalities and whether all passengers and crew are out of the water.

It is also unclear exactly what caused the collision. A source told the Guardian that it was possible the tanker had lost power before striking the cruise liner. Maritime experts speculated the incident may have been caused by either steerage failure or engine failure.

Officials said they would be holding a news conference, originally scheduled for 3pm CST, that has now been delayed. We are still waiting for it to begin.

Congressman Troy Carter, the representative for Louisiana’s 2nd congressional district, said in a statement that he was devastated by news of the crash.

“I am monitoring the situation closely and praying for the swift rescue of survivors and for all emergency crews responding to the situation,” he wrote on social media.

A video circulating around social media shows a different angle during the moment the ship was falling on its side into the water. Passengers can be seen climbing onto the balconies’ railing to avoid sliding into the river as the ship turned horizontally. Several uniformed people can be seen running away from the scene, likely to gather equipment that would help rescue efforts.

Martin J Davies, a maritime expert at Tulane University, told local news outlet Fox 8 that he suspected the incident was caused by either steerage failure or engine failure.

“This ship is headed upriver and for it to have lost the ability to avoid the moored vessel, there must have been some fairly substantial failure, with the steering gear or the propulsion of the ship to prevent them from avoiding the vessel on shore,” Davies said.

“Given that the pressure of the water going down river is sufficiently strong that the upbound ship would have been going fairly slowly,” he said. “So that’s my first thought upon looking at the footage.”

Davies also noted how close the vessels appear to be to the bridge above, suggesting the ship was lucky to have avoided also hitting the bridge. He said: “This is obviously a really bad incident, but it could also have struck the bridge, which it didn’t.”

That is so to assist responders as they “assess the situation and conduct response operations”, the coast guard’s statement said.

The New Orleans Regional Transit Authority said in a statement on social media that ferry service across the Mississippi River has been temporarily suspended as a precaution following the collision. Travelers are encouraged to seek alternate routes.

The ship then falls to its side towards the open water and begins sinking rapidly.

#175. US flight outage live updates: FAA grounds thousands of flights as Trump administration looks for cause of nationwide delays

Sean Duffy says technicians from Federal Aviation Administration are investigating; Trump’s homeland security secretary says ‘no indication as of yet’ of cyberattack

FAA issues nationwide ground-stop order, delaying or canceling nearly 7,000 flights

The US Federal ⁠Aviation Administration said in a statement that the nationwide halt to departures, and delays to landings, at all US airports that began at about 4pm ET on Friday was caused by “a temporary telecommunications outage”.

“Every flight has been accounted for and international flights will be handled on a case by case basis,” the FAA added.

Here is what we know about the rare nationwide “ground stop” issued by the FAA.

There is, as of now, no sign that the “telecommunications outage” that led to the sudden halt to all departures at US airports has been fixed.

It remains unclear what caused the outage, but, according to Markwayne Mullin, the former Oklahoma senator now leading the Department of Homeland Security, “there is no indication … that this was a nefarious attack”.

There have been 10,343 flight delays within, into or out of the US so far, according to the website FlightAware, and 1,479 cancellations.

This is just the second nationwide ​ground stop of all departures since the 11 September 2001 attacks. The previous one came in January 2023, when the FAA paused all ⁠domestic departures for less than two hours.

ASI, a Boston-based frontier AI company that was awarded an $875m software and AI contract in June by the Federal Aviation Administration wants everyone to know that its new air traffic management tool, known as SMART, which was deployed in September, is not to blame for the nationwide outage on Friday.

“The current ground stop is not based on or a result of the SMART system, any product of ASI or our integration with FAA and ATC,” the company said in a statement posted on social media.

In September, Sean Duffy, the former Fox News host leading the US Department of Transportation, starred in a social media video promoting the deployment of the Strategic Management of Airspace, Routing, and Trajectories, or SMART, system.

“We’re here to move at the speed of Tump,” Duffy said the video. “W’re leading the world in new technology.”

“Every flight has been accounted for and international flights will be handled on a case by case basis,” the FAA added.

Here is what we know about the rare nationwide “ground stop” issued by the FAA.

There is, as of now, no sign that the “telecommunications outage” that led to the sudden halt to all departures at US airports has been fixed.

It remains unclear what caused the outage, but, according to Markwayne Mullin, the former Oklahoma senator now leading the Department of Homeland Security, “there is no indication … that this was a nefarious attack”.

There have been 10,343 flight delays within, into or out of the US so far, according to the website FlightAware, and 1,479 cancellations.

This is just the second nationwide ​ground stop of all departures since the 11 September 2001 attacks. The previous one came in January 2023, when the FAA paused all ⁠domestic departures for less than two hours.

Nearly 7,000 flights across the United States were delayed or canceled on Friday, according to flight-tracking service FlightAware. Planes already ⁠in the air have been allowed to land, Reuters reports, but those on the ground could not take off.

The disruption is the latest and most dramatic sign of strain on a US air traffic ⁠control system beset by equipment and communications failures, controller shortages and ageing infrastructure.

The FAA’s ground stop order, halting departures from all US airports, runs through at least 11pm ET Friday.

Speaking to reporters outside the White House on the nationwide ground stop at all US airports, the US homeland security secretary, Markwayne Mullin, said: “Just to make sure we understand, there is no indication, and we had no reason to believe as of right now, that this was a threat to the United States. This was a telecommunication outage throughout FAA.”

The secretary added that there was no evidence “yet that this was a nefarious attack; it is just a telecommunication outage, as of right now”.

Asked whether it might have been caused by AI or a cyber-attack, Mullin incorrectly said that the last nationwide groundstop was “on 9/11” and sought to reassure the families of travelers that this outage was not part of a terrorist attack. “There is no reason to be concerned about your loved ones if they’re flying,” he said.

“As we investigate what caused this, you always got to assume that as many cyber-attacks that take place, that you make that assumption – so, but as of right now, that’s not known.”

After restoring communications so all airborne flights can land, Mullin said, “we’re going to continue to work to find out what the root of the cause is”.

“We’re not ruling out anything at this point,” Mullin added.

As we wait for clarity on what caused the nationwide groundstop of air travel to and from all US airports, you could do worse than read this weekend’s New York Times Magazine cover story from James Fallows, a veteran journalist and pilot about whether the US air travel system “is eroding margins of safety”.

Reports are steadily coming in from airports around the US, where passengers are confronting the sudden halt to all air traffic due to an as-yet-unexplained communications failure.

The Guardian’s own Tom Johnson, our head of audience development, sends along this dispatch from Ronald Reagan Washington national airport, known as DCA.

double quotation markHundreds of passengers wait for news in a packed terminal at Ronald Reagan Washington national airport. Gate agents, along with most travelers in the terminal, refreshed their phones looking for updates, and attempted to change their flights. Nearly every seat is filled, with some people sitting on the floor and using their luggage as makeshift desks for their laptops.

The exact cause of the nationwide air traffic control crisis in the US is not yet known, but, as our colleague Marina Dunbar reported, last week the Federal Aviation Administration announced that the first digital air traffic control towers in the US were officially operational, bringing new technology first to smaller airports in Florida and Alaska as the Trump administration presses ahead with updating the country’s aviation infrastructure.

The digital towers use advanced camera systems and digital displays to give air traffic controllers a continuous, 360-degree view of airport operations, including runways, taxiways and surrounding airspace, according to the agency.

The nationwide paralysis of the US air traffic control system, causing thousands of scheduled flights to be grounded and thousands more to be unable to land, has prompted a wave of anecdotal reports from passengers at airports.

This is just the second nationwide ​ground stop of all departures since the 11 September 2001, attacks. The previous one came in January 2023, when the FAA paused all ⁠domestic departures for less than two hours, Reuters reports.

Last year, ​Congress ​approved $12.5bn to fix ​the FAA’s ageing communications system, and ​on Friday ‌the FAA ​and ​White House asked Congress for another $30.1bn to address other issues.

According to the tracking website Flightradar24, there are currently “3,100 airborne aircraft bound for US airports amid nationwide ground stop”.

In addition to the grounding of all flights at US airports due to a communications issue affecting air traffic control, the US Federal ⁠Aviation Administration advised all airborne flights to circle along predetermined routes instead of landing, due to “equipment outages”.

The US transportation secretary, former Fox News host Sean Duffy, has confirmed that all flights are grounded at US airports “following a communication issue impacting all Air Route Traffic Control Centers”, in a statement posted on social media.

“FAA technicians are investigating the issue,” Duffy added. “We will provide immediate updates as we have them.”

Duffy’s post on the social media platform X appeared beneath one he pinned in late September, which includes a video of himself touting the introduction of a “new air traffic management tool that saves YOU time, cuts back DELAYS, and makes your flying experience BETTER”.

The US Federal ⁠Aviation Administration on ⁠Friday ​issued a national ground stop “due ⁠to extraordinary circumstance and reasons for safety” after ⁠the agency earlier reported ​serious ‌air traffic ‌control communications issues, Reuters reports.

Shortly before the ‌nationwide announcement, the FAA first halted traffic in Chicago due to air traffic control communications issues. ‌An agency notice posted on its website said: “All departing traffic should remain groundstopped until ​further notice.” The FAA said international flights will be managed ⁠on a case-by-case basis.

#172. Micron Technology: China probes US chip maker for cybersecurity risks as tech tension escalates

China has launched a cybersecurity probe into Micron Technology, one of America’s largest memory chip makers, in apparent retaliation after US allies in Asia and Europe announced new restrictions on the sale of key technology to Beijing.

The Cyberspace Administration of China (CAC) will review products sold by Micron in the country, according to a statement by the watchdog late on Friday.

The move is aimed at “ensuring the security of key information infrastructure supply chains, preventing cybersecurity risks caused by hidden product problems, and maintaining national security,” it noted.

It came on the same day that Japan, a US ally, said it would restrict the export of advanced chip manufacturing equipment to countries including China, following similar moves by the United States and the Netherlands.

Washington and its allies have announced curbs on China’s semiconductor industry, which strike at the heart of Beijing’s bid to become a tech superpower.

Last month, the Netherlands also unveiled new restrictions on overseas sales of semiconductor technology, citing the need to protect national security. In October, the United States banned Chinese companies from buying advanced chips and chipmaking equipment without a license.

Micron told CNN it was aware of the review.

“We are in communication with the CAC and are cooperating fully,” it said, adding that it stands by the security of its products. “Micron’s product shipments, engineering, manufacturing, sales and other functions are operating as normal.”

Shares in Micron sank 4.4% on Wall Street Friday following the news, the biggest drop in more than three months. On Monday, they closed another 1.2% lower. Micron derives more than 10% of its revenue from China.

In an earlier filing, the Idaho-based company had warned of such risks.

“The Chinese government may restrict us from participating in the China market or may prevent us from competing effectively with Chinese companies,” it said last week.

Japan joins the US and Europe in chipmaking curbs on China

China has strongly criticized restrictions on tech exports, saying last month it “firmly opposes” such measures.

In efforts to boost growth and job creation, Beijing is seeking to woo foreign investments as it grapples with mounting economic challenges. The newly minted premier Li Qiang and several top economic officials have been rolling out the welcome wagon for global CEOs and promising they would “provide a good environment and services.”

But Beijing has also exerted growing pressure on foreign companies to bring them into line with its agenda.

Last month, authorities closed the Beijing office of Mintz Group, a US corporate intelligence firm, and detained five local staff.

Days earlier, they suspended Deloitte’s operations in Beijing for three months and imposed a fine of $31 million over alleged lapses in its work auditing a state-owned distressed debt manager.

CNN’s Michelle Toh, Junko Ogura, Sandi Sidhu and Chris Lau contributed to the article.

#169. Families clear homes of David Walliams books after sexual offence allegations

Parents take action while several independent bookshops say they had already stopped stocking his titles

In Sarah’s house, David Walliams has been a bedtime staple for years. Her children, aged eight, six and four, have dressed up as his characters for World Book Day – they love the over-the-top, unusual names and illustrations.

Now, all the books are going. “I’ll probably take them away and replace them with other authors,” says Sarah, a mother in west London. “Sooner rather than later, so they don’t realise.” If any of her children ask why, she will tell them “that his choices aren’t great and that I think we should be reading other books”.

This week a joint Guardian and ITV News investigation alleged that the children’s author used his position to pursue young women and girls for sex, including girls as young as 15. Walliams denies the allegations. HarperCollins stopped publishing new Walliams books last December and dropped his entire backlist in September.

For many independent children’s booksellers, the news only confirmed a decision made long ago.

Helen Tamblyn-Saville, owner of Wonderland Bookshop in Retford, Nottinghamshire, stopped stocking him in 2020. “They didn’t fit the ethos and values of my shop,” she says. Walliams’s books “punched down”, she believes, citing what she sees as “themes of racism, misogyny and classism” in his writings.

In 2021, HarperCollins removed a story about a Chinese boy – Brian Wong, Who Was Never, Ever Wrong – from future editions of The World’s Worst Children after complaints that it relied on harmful racial stereotypes.

In 2025, Tamblyn-Saville turned down an invitation to an event with him. “It feels very much as if the golden goose had to be protected, despite the clear red flags.”

Griffin Books in Penarth, south Wales, dropped his books about 18 months ago over “the low quality of the writing and his frequent use of offensive stereotypes”.

The Chicken and Frog bookshop in Brentwood, Essex, has not stocked him for a decade. Urmi, owner of Pickled Pepper Books in north London, has a blunter opinion: “We heard [rumours] about David Walliams at least five years ago and at that point chose to not stock his books,” she says. She believes that “everyone in the industry knew … so anyone that’s only just taking books off the shelf is only interested in profit”.

Mainstream stores have moved quickly. The Works, a budget retailer, said it had removed all David Walliams titles from sale and TG Jones confirmed it no longer sold his books in store or online. Waterstones said it was talking to HarperCollins about returning “the small number of copies which remain in stock in a few of our shops”.

Families are clearing their shelves too. One parent walked past a neighbourhood book-swap box this week and found it stuffed full of Walliams titles. Another mother joked that she would be burning hers, saying Walliams was “a knock-off Roald Dahl anyway”. Sarah hasn’t decided what to do with hers: giving them to a charity shop just puts them “back into the system”.

Walliams’ sales have been falling sharply – but they have not disappeared. Retailers sold 7,497 Walliams books over the past four weeks, worth £48,400, according to NielsenIQ BookData figures reported by The Bookseller.

In the same period last year, when he had a new book out, Walliams sold 102,000. Even so, almost 108,000 copies of his books have been sold so far this year, worth more than £702,000, and his titles have made more than £153m since 2008.

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

Public libraries are also facing a difficult decision. “This is a very sensitive issue involving deeply disturbing allegations, and some people may find the continued presence of these books upsetting,” said Isobel Hunter, CEO of the charity Libraries Connected.

“But we also have a long-established legal principle within the sector that all books should be available to the public unless the content itself is unlawful.

“So most library services have said that the books will remain in their collections until they are naturally weeded out and withdrawn under existing stock policy. That doesn’t mean, of course, that libraries are obliged to promote them or have them on display.”

Anecdotally, she said, demand had “dropped significantly in recent years”.

Some schools appear to be taking a harder line – several teachers told the Guardian they were removing Walliams books from their classroom shelves.

For Sarah, the principle is simple: “Kids need to understand that actions have consequences. If something’s not right, then we need to act upon it.”

#167. Walliams allegations prompt calls for staggered age of consent at 16 and 17

Barrister say law does not adequately recognise power imbalance between young people and much older adults

Allegations against Russell Brand and, separately, David Walliams, which he denies, have led to calls for greater legal protection for 16- and 17-year-olds from sexual exploitation by older adults.

A staggered consent law is one route favoured by campaigners, supported by some Labour MPs. Claire Waxman, the victims’ commissioner for England and Wales, has called for “serious conversations” on the potential for legislative change.

The barrister Charlotte Proudman, who specialises in law regarding violence against women and girls, said: “Staggered, or graduated, age of consent means that the law takes account of the age difference between sexual partners, rather than treating 16 as the point at which a child can legally consent to sexual activity with someone of any age.

“A sexual relationship between a 16-year-old and an 18-year-old is fundamentally different from one between a 16-year-old girl and a 50-year-old man.”

A 16-year-old cannot buy alcohol or cigarettes, but someone decades older can legally have sex with them. “The law does not adequately recognise the significant power imbalance between children and much older adults, particularly where grooming, financial dependence, celebrity status or emotional manipulation are involved,” Proudman said.

A move to staggered consent “would place responsibility on the older adult”, she said, rather than requiring a teenage complainant to demonstrate grooming, coercion or exploitation.

“This is not about denying young people sexual autonomy,” she said. “It is about recognising that consent must be understood in the context of age, maturity, vulnerability and power.”

The Close the Consent Gap campaign proposes a staggered age of consent making it a criminal offence for a 16- or 17-year-old to consent to sexual activity with anyone more than three years their senior.

“For example, a consensual relationship between two teenagers close in age would remain lawful, whereas sexual activity between a 16-year-old and a 35-year-old would be prohibited,” said the campaign’s founder, Layla Stone.

“Every other framework of the law recognises 16- and 17-year-olds as children. We want the law to recognise children – 16 and 17 years old – as children who need to be protected in all frameworks of the law.”

Stone said there could be a “huge power imbalance” between a 16-year-old living at home and a 21-year-old with money and independence.

The law on the issue in the rest of the world is complex and varied. Some countries have extra safeguards built in for 16- and 17-year-olds. Canada, for example, recognises age difference as a factor in determining exploitation for this age group. For 14- and 15-year-olds, Canadian law criminalises any sexual activity if a partner is five or more years older.

Prof Roger Ingham, the director of the Centre for Sexual Health Research at the University of Southampton, said: “My question, as a researcher, is: what is the evidence that it actually works or doesn’t work to protect young people?”

He said there was no strong evidence that different countries with different ages had completely different patterns of sexual behaviour or outcomes. But he said it was a difficult question to answer “because a lot of these cases are simply not reported”.

Ingham said “tinkering with the law” could divert attention from other efforts, including better sex education, and risked young people avoiding seeking contraceptive advice, or other support they needed, if their relationship was deemed illegal.

Dr Sophie King-Hill, an associate professor at the University of Birmingham researching sexual behaviour of children and young people, said people were “looking for fixes, looking for blame, looking for gaps in the law”, but she also questioned where the evidence was that it worked.

“If we’re looking at preventing grooming and preventing young people being exploited, we’ve got to be really careful not to confuse this changing of the law with solving the problem,” she said.

“Grooming, coercion, manipulation and abuse of power don’t suddenly begin at 16. I can see where it’s coming from. People feel like they need to do something, and I’m not necessarily opposed to it in its totality, but I think it’s really naive to see it as such a solution to something that’s really complex and complicated within society.”

King-Hill said it could create a false sense of protection for young people, and it risked children and young people not coming forward “because they’ll be worried they’ve broken the law”.

“I think from my research perspective and the work that I’ve carried out, you’ve got to do things like this really carefully, because you can inadvertently make things worse or oversimplify a very complex and complicated problem.”

Elon Musk's Twitter promised a purge of blue check marks. Instead he singled out one account

#162. Probation officers in England and Wales vote to strike over pay and workloads

Move deals blow to government’s plans to release thousands of prisoners early to tackle prison overcrowding

The government’s plans to release thousands of prisoners early were dealt a blow on Friday after probation officers across England and Wales voted to strike over pay and excessive workloads.

Members of the union, Napo, backed overtime restrictions and other forms of action short of a strike, which is likely to start on 23 November. Plans are being made for strike action to follow at a later stage.

The union, whose members work across probation offices and in family courts, said it launched its ballot because of an “inadequate” 5% pay offer from ministers and a failure to cut workloads.

Probation officers have come under increasing pressure since the launch of the latest early release scheme in October, which is meant to ease overcrowding in prisons.

They are responsible for monitoring offenders’ behaviour, overseeing their rehabilitation, monitoring their electronic tags and recalling them to prison for any offences or licence breaches, such as entering an exclusion or restriction zone protecting victims.

By the end of the year, about 2,500 offenders, some of whom are sex offenders, domestic abusers and stalkers, will be released under the scheme.

The union had sought a 12% increase. Napo general secretary Ian Lawrence said: “The high turnout by our members and the result vindicates the campaign to achieve a pay rise to make up lost ground in comparative pay with other staff employed within HMPPS [HM Prison and Probation Service].

“The vote also reflects serious anger at the state of probation workloads, which were already in crisis before the latest version of the prisoner early release scheme.”

Napo said probation wages have “stagnated” since 2010, with reception staff on a salary just above the minimum wage – the lowest in the civil service.

On pay, Napo members voted by 85% in favour of action short of strike action and 74% in favour of strike action. In a ballot on workloads, the vote was 87% in favour of action short of strike action and 75% in favour of strikes.

The Ministry of Justice is recruiting an additional 1,300 trainee probation officers for the 2026-27 financial year as part of a £700m investment package extending through 2028-29.

Sign up for the Breaking News UK email to get newsletter alerts direct to your inbox

The Institute for Government said slow progress on recruitment means probation practitioners were still overstretched and ill-equipped to absorb additional demand.

It said there had been a serious staff shortage in probation for several years, with the number of probation officers consistently 25-30% below target staffing. As of March 2026, nine probation delivery units (8% of the total) had a vacancy rate of 40% or more.

A Ministry of Justice spokesperson said: “Probation staff do a vital job protecting the public, which is why we have put a strong offer on the table, giving every worker a further 5% pay rise, which follows the biggest probation pay increase in over a decade alongside record investment. We’re also recruiting an additional 1,300 officers to significantly cut workloads.

“While we are disappointed that some have voted against this competitive deal, it will not change our first responsibility of protecting the public and we have robust contingency plans in place to ensure offenders are still subject to the same rigorous supervision.”

#160. Erdogan's political fate may be determined by Turkey's Kurds

Editor’s Note:  A version of this story first appeared in CNN’s Meanwhile in the Middle East newsletter, a three-times-a-week look inside the region’s biggest stories. Sign up here.

Turkey’s persecuted pro-Kurdish party has emerged as a kingmaker in the country’s upcoming election, playing a decisive role that may just tip the balance enough to unseat two-decade ruler Recep Tayyip Erdogan.

In a key setback to the Turkish president and leader of the Justice and Development Party (AK Party), the pro-Kurdish Peoples’ Democratic Party (HDP) last month announced that it would not put forward its own presidential candidate, a move analysts say allows its supporters to vote for Erdogan’s main rival.

“We are facing a turning point that will shape the future of Turkey and (its) society,” said the HDP in a statement on March 23. “To fulfill our historical responsibility against the one-man rule, we will not field a presidential candidate in (the) May 14 elections.”

It is a twist of irony for the Turkish strongman, who spent the better half of the past decade cracking down on the party after it began chipping away at his voter base. Its former leader Selahattin Demirtas has been in prison for nearly seven years and the party faces possible closure by a court for suspected collusion with the militant Kurdistan Workers’ Party (PKK) and affiliated groups. But its influence may nonetheless determine the course of Turkey’s politics.

The HDP’s decision not to field a candidate came just three days after head of the Republican People’s Party (CHP) Kemal Kilicdaroglu, Erdogan’s main rival, visited the party’s co-chairs. He told reporters that the solution to Turkey’s problems, “including the Kurdish problem” lies in parliament,” according to Turkish media.

Kilicdaroglu, who represents the six-party Nation Alliance opposition bloc, is the strongest contender to run against Erdogan in years. And while the HDP hasn’t yet announced whether it will put its weight behind him, analysts say it is the kingmaker in the elections.

Erdogan's 'polar opposite' wants to replace him as president of Turkey

“It was a carefully crafted political discourse,” Hisyar Ozsoy, deputy co-chair of the HDP and a member of parliament from the predominantly Kurdish province of Diyarbakir, told CNN. “We are not going to have our own candidate, and we will leave it to the international community to interpret it the way they wish.”

Experts say the crackdown on the HDP is rooted in the threat it poses to Erdogan politically, as well as its position as one of the main parties representing Turkey’s Kurds, an ethnic minority from which a separatist militant movement has emerged.

The party and the Kurdish people have had a complicated relationship with Erdogan. The leader courted the Kurds in earlier years by granting them more rights and reversing restrictions on the use of their language. Relations with the HDP were also cordial once, as Erdogan worked with the party on a brief peace process with the PKK.

But ties between Erdogan and the HDP later turned sour, and the HDP fell under a sweeping crackdown aimed at the PKK and their affiliates.

Kurds are the biggest minority in Turkey, making up between 15% and 20% of the population, according to Minority Rights Group International.

It is unclear if the HDP will endorse Kilicdaroglu, but analysts say that the deliberate distance may be beneficial for the opposition candidate.

The accusations against the HDP place it in a precarious position during the elections. It currently faces a case in Turkey’s Constitutional Court over suspected ties to the PKK, which is designated as a terrorist group by Turkey, the United States and the European Union. Knowing it may be banned at any moment, its candidates are running under the Green Left Party in parliament.

If the opposition is seen as allying with the HDP, Erdogan’s AK Party may use its influence in the media to discredit it as being pro-PKK, said Murat Somer, a political science professor at Koc University in Istanbul and author of Return to Point Zero, a book on the Turkish-Kurdish question in Turkey.

The HDP’s threat to Erdogan’s hold on power became apparent after the June 2015 election, the first general election it participated in. It won 13% of the seats, denying the ruling AK Party its majority for the first time since 2002. Erdogan, however, called a snap election five months later, which led to a drop in the HDP’s support to 10.7%, as well as the restoration of the AK Party’s overall majority.

“They are a kingmaker in these elections because the HDP gets about half of the votes of the Kurdish population in Turkey,” said Somer, adding that the other, more conservative Kurdish voters have traditionally voted for Erdogan’s AK Party. And last month, the Free Cause Party (HUDA-PAR), a tiny Kurdish-Islamist party announced support for Erdogan in the elections. The party has never won seats in parliament.

Erdogan's political fate may rest on his response to the earthquake

The HDP knows that its position is key to the outcome of next month’s vote, but that it’s also in a delicate situation.

“We want to play the game wisely, and we need to be very careful,” said Ozsoy, adding that the party wants to avoid a “contaminated political climate” where the elections are polarized “between a very ugly ultra-nationalist discourse against Kilicdaroglu and others.”

The party was founded in 2012 with a number of aims, said Ozsoy, one of which was “peaceful and democratic resolution of the Kurdish conflict.”

Somer said that the party was seen to be “an initiative” of the PKK, which later led to a heavy government crackdown on it in the name of counterterrorism.

Its former leader Demirtas remains an influential figure.

The Turkish government has been trying to link the HDP to the PKK but has so far failed to prove “a real connection,” said Asli Aydintasbas, a visiting fellow at the Brookings Institution in Washington, DC.

A post-Erdogan Turkey may give some breathing space to the Kurds and Kurdish-dominated parties in Turkey, Aydintasbas told CNN, noting that many Kurdish voters have recently left Erdogan’s camp. “For HDP, this is more than just an ideological choice,” she said. “It’s a matter of survival.”

Ozsoy says his party understands what’s at stake, not only for Turkey’s Kurds but for all its minorities.

“We are aware of our responsibility here. We are aware of our role. We know we are in a kingmaker position,” the HDP lawmaker said.

He revisits classic research on the illusion of control and shows how often programs are launched without strong evidence they work.
